Measured, and self-caught one command short of it: a coordinator probed a freeze 25 seconds in, got the empty log, no `build.mjs` process and a zero-entry submodule, wrote down that the launch had died, and was stopped only because the delete was GATED on the liveness check rather than chained after it — `/proc/<pid>/cwd` named the live clone and the compiler beneath it. **So the gate is not belt-and-braces over a probe you trust; it is the ONLY thing standing between a normal startup and a destroyed snapshot**, which is §A-DESTRUCTIVE-STEP-IS-GATED-BY-THE-CHECK'S-EXIT-STATUS earning its keep in the case its author did not foresee. And the general form, which outlives the freeze: **a probe for LIVENESS must not be a probe for a SIDE EFFECT that the subject produces only in a later stage** — asking whether the output exists answers a question about progress, never about existence. **AND THE RULE ABOVE WAS BROKEN BY ITS OWN AUTHOR ONE TURN AFTER LANDING IT, IN THE ONE WAY THAT DEFEATS EVERY CHECK: THE ANSWER DID NOT GATE THE ACTION.** The check was run. It printed six live processes with their working directory inside the doomed path. The `rm -rf` then ran anyway, because the question and the deletion were written as ONE unconditional command and the shell does not care what the loop above it printed. This is §AN-ASSERT-WHOSE-TWO-SIDES-CANNOT-DISAGREE arriving in a shell instead of in C: a verification whose result nothing branches on is not a weak check, it is a NON-check that produces a reassuring transcript. **THE TRANSCRIPT IS THE HAZARD** — a later reader, and the author, sees the check and its correct answer sitting right there and concludes the decision was made on it, when nothing consumed it. Measured, and only the author's own measurement runs were destroyed, which is luck rather than diligence: the same command against a peer's directory would have been the banned destroy with the evidence of care printed above it. So a destructive step is GATED BY THE CHECK'S EXIT STATUS or it is a separate command run after reading the answer — `if <check>; then <destroy>; fi`, never `<check>; <destroy>` — and the same test applies to every verification you write: **ask what the program does differently depending on the answer, and if the answer is nothing, you have written a comment with a pipeline in it.** — and its neighbour, a peer's commit that happens to LOOK like yours, is a false negative that certifies a commit nobody read. **WHEN IT IS NOT, THE ANSWER IS NEVER AN AMEND OR A REVERT** — a swept-in diff that is COHERENT and whole-landed is pushed as it stands, because reverting it destroys a peer's work and amending it rewrites history under them; state the mis-attribution in the next commit's message and in the report. What must be checked before pushing is not authorship but COHERENCE: a cross-boundary diff that arrived half-staged is the one case that must not go out, and that is a question about the content, which you can answer, rather than about who wrote it, which you cannot. A `git add` you leave sitting is not a private note, it is an offer. **AND THE POST-COMMIT READ IS SCOPED TO ONE COMMIT WHILE A PUSH IS SCOPED TO AN ANCESTRY, SO THE ONE VERIFICATION THIS FILE PRESCRIBES IS STRUCTURALLY BLIND TO WHAT A PUSH ACTUALLY PUBLISHES.** `git show --stat <sha>` answers about exactly the commit you made, which is what makes it the right check for a sweep — and `git push <sha>:<ref>` publishes that commit AND EVERY COMMIT BENEATH IT. In a shared checkout peers commit onto the same branch between your commits, so the set you publish is not the set you read, and nothing in the read says so: it is correct, it is about your commit, and it is silent about the others riding out under it. This is not the index sweep — nothing is mis-attributed and every commit keeps its own author and message — it is a PUBLICATION you did not look at, and its hazard is the one §A-CROSS-BOUNDARY-DIFF names: a peer's HALF-LANDED commit, coherent-looking on its own, going out because it happened to be an ancestor. Measured, three times in one hour by one agent: two pushes each carried a peer's fixture commit the pusher had never opened, and a third would have carried a peer's 65-line change to a file the pusher was not working in. **THE CHECK IS ONE COMMAND AND IT GOES BEFORE THE PUSH, NOT AFTER**: `git log --oneline <remote-ref>..<sha>` lists exactly what you are about to publish. **AND THE INSPECTION AND THE PUSH MUST NAME THE SAME `<sha>`, CAPTURED ONCE, BECAUSE TWO EVALUATIONS OF A MOVING REF RE-OPEN THE RACE THE INSPECTION EXISTS TO CLOSE.** This rule already says `<sha>` and it is obeyed by reading `origin/main..HEAD` and then pushing `$(git rev-parse HEAD)` — which is not one check followed by one push, it is TWO QUESTIONS ASKED OF A REF OTHER LANES ADVANCE BETWEEN THEM. The list you read is then a list of what you WOULD have published, and the set you publish is unread, which is exactly the state the check was written to prevent, reached by performing it. Measured: a coordinator inspected five commits, found them coherent, and published SIX — the extra was a peer's one-line include removal that landed in the interval and was harmless, which is luck and not method. So capture the SHA into a variable FIRST, inspect that variable, push that variable, and read back by that variable — `HEAD` appears nowhere in the sequence. The tell is that you typed `HEAD` twice in one procedure and expected the same answer. **AND A VARIABLE ASSIGNED TWICE IS NOT A CAPTURED SHA — THE SECOND ASSIGNMENT RE-OPENS THE RACE THE FIRST ONE CLOSED, AND THE PROCEDURE STILL LOOKS OBEYED.** The rule above is followed by writing `SHA=$(git rev-parse HEAD)` once and using `$SHA` thereafter, and it is DEFEATED by any later line that re-derives the same variable — a fallback when the first spelling looked wrong, a re-read "to be safe", a second capture in the push command. The transcript then contains a captured SHA, an inspection of a captured SHA, and a push of a captured SHA, and they are not all the same SHA. Measured, by the author of the correction, twice in one session: an inspection printed one commit, a fumbled construction of the variable was followed by a bare re-capture in the same command, and the push published a peer's commit that had landed in the interval — coherent both times, which is luck and is exactly what the rule exists to stop being load-bearing. **THE TELL IS PURELY SYNTACTIC AND COSTS NOTHING TO CHECK: count the assignments to the variable, and count the occurrences of `rev-parse HEAD` in the whole procedure.** More than one of either means the capture is decorative. And the corollary for a fumbled capture specifically — if the value looks wrong, ECHO IT AND STOP; do not repair it with a fresh evaluation, because a repair that re-reads the ref has silently changed the subject of every check above it. **AND A PUBLICATION TAKES THE SAME GATE AS A DESTRUCTION, WHICH THIS RULE STOPS ONE STEP SHORT OF SAYING.** It ends at *the check goes before the push, not after*, and that is satisfied by a command that inspects and then pushes — which is `<check>; <publish>` and therefore the NON-CHECK §A-DESTRUCTIVE-STEP-IS-GATED-BY-THE-CHECK'S-EXIT-STATUS already forbids, arriving at the one irreversible outward act this file had exempted by omission. A push is not undoable: the rules here forbid rewriting published history to fix attribution, so what goes out stays out, and an inspection nothing branches on leaves exactly the reassuring transcript that rule names — the list of commits and the correct verdict sitting in the output, above a push that would have run whatever the list said. Measured, by the author of the correction it violates, one turn after making it: an inspection printed a peer's commit, the push in the same command published it unread, and the commit turned out coherent — which is luck, and luck is what the gate exists to stop being load-bearing. **THE CAPTURED-SHA DISCIPLINE DOES NOT COVER THIS AND READING IT AS IF IT DOES IS THE TRAP**: capturing the SHA once closes the moving-ref race, so the set published IS the set inspected, and that correctness is exactly what makes the missing gate invisible. Two different defects, one procedure; fixing the race does not fix the branch. So the inspection is its own command, whose OUTPUT YOU READ, and the push is a later one — or the two are joined by a condition that can actually refuse. **The tell is arithmetic: you can name what your inspection would have had to print to stop you, and nothing in the command could have acted on it.** Read the list, open anything you do not recognise, and look for COHERENCE rather than authorship — authorship is not yours to police, and a commit whose halves did not land together is the one that must not go out, which is a question about content you can answer. **Never rewrite pushed history to fix attribution** — an amend or a revert-and-recommit under other agents is strictly worse than a commit whose message under-describes it; state the mis-attribution in the report instead, and rely on the code carrying its own explanation at every site. **AND THE FILTER ITSELF HAS AN ORDINAL IN IT, WHICH IS THE LAST PLACE THIS DEFECT HIDES.** Everything above is about WHEN you stage; this is about HOW you choose, and it defeats the discipline while looking like it. A filtered stage picks hunks out of a diff, and the obvious way to name one is its POSITION — the third hunk, the one at line 2635. But a hunk index is an ORDINAL OVER A SET OTHER LANES MUTATE, so it is §AN-INDEX-NAMES-A-THING-ONLY-WHILE-THE-SET-IS-FIXED wearing a patch: a peer's edit landing between the `git diff` you read and the `git diff` you filter renumbers every hunk after theirs, and your carefully-filtered patch then stages THEIR hunk under YOUR message. It is quiet in the usual way — the patch applies, the commit succeeds, the file list looks right, and only the CONTENT is wrong. Measured twice in one session, independently: one lane's positional pick took a peer's `#include` into its commit when a file was edited between its hunk listing and its hunk selection, and a second reported "hunk selection by line number failed twice" and switched to content markers on its own. So a filtered stage selects by CONTENT — a distinctive string from the hunk you wrote — never by position, and the post-commit read stays mandatory because content matching is not proof either. The tell that you are about to do this: you are about to write a number that refers to a diff you produced in a DIFFERENT command than the one you are about to run.
- **THE WORKING TREE IS SHARED STATE TOO, SO `git reset --hard`, `git checkout -- <paths>`, `git stash` AND `git clean` ARE BANNED — they destroy other agents' uncommitted work with NO record and NO recovery.** The staging rule above is only the WRITE side: it says an index entry is a publication. This is the READ side, and it is the half that keeps being learned the expensive way — THREE separate lanes lost complete diffs to one `reset --hard` in a single session, one of them a nine-file change and another a large threat-model rewrite, and the lane that ran it was tidying its OWN state and could not see whose work it was taking. That is the asymmetry that makes this a rule rather than a caution: the operation is local and ordinary in a private checkout, and in a shared one its blast radius is invisible from the command line. Nothing in git records it — there is no reflog entry for a working-tree file that was never committed, so the loss is discovered by the victim, later, as an absence. **THE COROLLARY IS THAT HOLDING WORK IN THE WORKING TREE IS NOT SAFE EITHER, and the two halves of that are not in tension — they are the same fact.** Staging early offers your hunks to the next commit; leaving them unstaged offers them to the next checkout. The resolution is not to pick a safer place to wait, it is NOT TO WAIT: reach a committable state and commit it, in one uninterrupted operation, rather than accumulating a large diff that any peer's ordinary command can erase. A diff you cannot land yet is one to make smaller, never one to park. If a peer's state genuinely must be restored, that is a conversation with the agent that owns it, never a command that silently answers for them.
- **AND THE ONE FILE `git diff origin/main` CANNOT SEE IS THE GITLINK, SO THE STALE CHECKOUT ABOVE HAS A FORM WHOSE PRESCRIBED REMEDY IS BLIND TO IT.** The rule above ends in an instrument — confirm `git diff origin/main -- <path>` holds only your hunks — and that instrument answers about FILE CONTENT. A submodule is not content in the superproject; it is a recorded COMMIT ID, and when the working checkout of `engine/qjs` sits at an older commit than the one `origin/main` records, `git diff origin/main -- engine/qjs` is **SILENT**. Not quiet-because-clean: silent about a difference that exists. A commit made from that state moves the gitlink BACKWARD, and a backward gitlink is not a no-op — it REVERTS every submodule commit between the two, under a message about something else, which is the shared-index sweep arriving through a pointer instead of through a file. Measured: a lane's checkout was one commit stale, its first parent commit would have reverted a peer's landed citation fix, and the diff-against-`origin/main` check that CLAUDE.md prescribes reported nothing wrong. It was caught only by the OTHER rule — reading `git show --stat HEAD` AFTER committing — which is the second time that post-commit read has been the thing that worked when a pre-commit check could not. So: **the gitlink is verified by reading the ID, never by diffing the path.** `git rev-parse origin/main:engine/qjs` against `git -C engine/qjs rev-parse HEAD` before you commit, and `git show HEAD -- engine/qjs` after — and if the two commits differ, establish which is an ANCESTOR of which, because behind and ahead look identical in a pointer and only one of them is yours to move.
- **A CROSS-BOUNDARY DIFF IS ALREADY HALF-DEPLOYED THE MOMENT IT IS WRITTEN, SO AN UNLANDED ONE IS NOT NEUTRAL STATE — IT IS THE WORST OF THE THREE STATES, AND WAITING IS CHOOSING IT.** The rule above says an unstaged diff is exposed to the next checkout; this is the sharper half, and it is about the TREE BEING A RUNNING SYSTEM rather than a pile of pending work. The two zones do not go live at the same instant: the trusted zone's JavaScript is INTERPRETED FROM THE TREE, so an edit to it is deployed on WRITE, while the engine's C is live only after somebody builds. A change that spans the seam therefore deploys ITSELF ASYMMETRICALLY — the JS half starts speaking a protocol the shipped WASM does not implement, on a field the engine does not yet state, and the very DCHECK the diff added to make the contract explicit is what fires, in the half that IS live, naming a contract the half that is NOT live would have satisfied. Whole-unlanded and whole-landed are both coherent; the state in between is the one where the product does not run and the assert points away from the cause. **SO THE QUESTION IS NOT WHO OWNS IT, IT IS WHICH HALF IS LIVE ON WRITE** — ask that at every change touching both zones, and land the two halves together or not at all. When the author does not come back, the diff is READ, VERIFIED and LANDED by whoever is standing there, with the attribution stated in the message: history is never rewritten to fix attribution, and the tree is never reverted to "restore" cleanliness, which is the banned destroy wearing a tidy-up. **AND "DOES NOT COME BACK" IS THE PART THAT HAS NO INSTRUMENT, BECAUSE AN MTIME CANNOT TELL A FINISHED DIFF FROM A MID-EDIT ONE.** The rule above turns on whether an author is returning, and every cheap probe for that answers about the WRITER rather than about the WORK: an mtime a minute cold looks identical to a lane that is thinking, no process holds the file (an editor writes and closes), and a lane can silently exceed its brief, so a scope list is not proof either. **AND THE TWO MISTAKES ARE NOT SYMMETRIC.** Landing a half-written file publishes a peer's incomplete thought under your name — the shape that once had a mid-scan read report 98 broken contracts — while REFUSING to land a finished one leaves it in the state this bullet already calls the worst of the three, and in the trusted zone that state is not merely at risk from the next checkout, it is DEPLOYED, since that JavaScript is interpreted from the tree. **SO THE QUESTION IS ANSWERED FROM THE DIFF AND NEVER FROM THE CLOCK, AND IT IS ANSWERABLE: A FINISHED DIFF CLOSES ITS OWN LOOSE ENDS.** It retires the residual that deferred it, rewrites the comment its own change made false, and adds the assert its new read requires — so the reader half and the writer half and the prose all arrive together. A HALF-WRITTEN one is visibly lopsided in a way no clock is needed to see: it reads a field it has not asserted, asserts one it does not read, or leaves standing a residual saying the very thing it just did was deferred. **AND THE SHARPER HALF IS THAT A GOOD DIFF HANDS A STRANGER THE COMMAND THAT SETTLES IT.** Measured, and it is why this one was landable by someone who did not write it: a popup card began reading two engine fields, and the comment retiring its own deferral said the check had been made *by CONTENT and never by timestamp* — naming the artifact and the name to look for. Re-running that with a NEGATIVE CONTROL took one command and answered both halves at once: the two names occur in the shipped wasm and an invented one does not, so the producer was live in the ARTIFACT rather than merely in source, which is the whole of what §A-CROSS-BOUNDARY-DIFF asks. **WHAT MADE IT CHECKABLE WAS THAT THE AUTHOR WROTE A FALSIFIABLE CLAIM INSTEAD OF A REASSURANCE**, and that is the obligation this creates in the other direction: when you defer a cross-boundary half, say in the code what observation would retire the deferral, so the person who finds it later can run that observation rather than having to reconstruct your reasoning or wait for you. The tell that you are about to land something you should not: you can say the file looks complete and you cannot name one loose end it closed. **AND THE AXIS IN THAT RULE'S OWN NAME IS THE WRONG ONE, WHICH ITS AUTHOR ESTABLISHED BY LANDING IT AND THEN BEING TOLD.** "Does not come back" reads as a question about ABSENCE, and finishedness and presence are INDEPENDENT: the diff that produced this paragraph was written by a lane that was still RUNNING — it had completed that file and moved to its next step — and it confirmed afterwards that landing it was correct. So an author being live is not evidence a file is mid-edit, exactly as an author being gone is not evidence it is finished. Ask only whether the DIFF closes its own loose ends; who is holding the keyboard answers nothing either way, and reaching for the author is how the cheap wrong probes get used. **AND THE DEFERRAL SUCH A DIFF RETIRES IS THE ONE STALE CLAIM IN THIS PROJECT THAT NO GIT COMMAND CAN SEE, BECAUSE IT ROTS AGAINST AN ARTIFACT RATHER THAN AGAINST THE TREE.** Every staleness check this file prescribes is git-based — grep the entry at `origin/main`, `git show <the-commit-that-wrote-the-clause>:<file>`, `git merge-base --is-ancestor` — and all of them answer correctly and uselessly about a residual whose claim is *the shipped binary does not implement this yet*. That sentence is falsified by a BUILD, and a build leaves no commit; the source it was deferring against can be byte-identical at both revisions, so every git answer is "unchanged" while the fact has flipped. It is therefore the residual most likely to outlive its reason, and it does so wearing a correct citation of the rule that justified it. **THE CHECK IS BY CONTENT IN THE ARTIFACT, WITH A NEGATIVE CONTROL, AND IT IS TWO COMMANDS**: look for the name in the built binary — never at a timestamp, which orders two files and says nothing about what is in them — and look for an invented name beside it, so a zero means absent rather than *my probe never reached this*. Then confirm the producer's own sources are byte-identical between the artifact's stamped revision and `HEAD`, which is the one part git does answer. So when you write a cross-boundary deferral, NAME THE OBSERVATION THAT RETIRES IT rather than the reason it exists — a reason has to be re-derived by whoever finds it, and an observation can simply be run. **AND ASKING PERMISSION IS NOT AN EXEMPTION.** A question whose answer does not change what a careful engineer would do is deferral with a question mark on it, and every turn it is asked the half-deployed state persists — the escalation that is worth making is the one about INTENT, never the one about risk this file has already decided. **AND THE SCOPE YOU GIVE SUCH A DIFF IS A CLAIM ABOUT THE CALL PATH, WHICH IS THE ONE PART A CONCEPTUAL MODEL OF THE SEAM GETS WRONG.** Everything above assumes you know which files the two halves are. A seam is usually pictured as one hop — *the bridge relays to the ABI* — and the real path can be four, each hop a place the diff must land. Scoped short, the diff is not INCOMPLETE, it is INCOHERENT: the halves that do land describe a protocol no other hop implements. **AND ONE OF THOSE HOPS IS OFTEN MACHINE-CHECKED IN BOTH DIRECTIONS, WHICH TURNS A NARROW SCOPE INTO A RED BUILD FOR EVERY LANE.** Measured: a coordinator scoped a chunk-delivery entry as three files — the ABI header, its implementation, the bridge. The lane traced it and found **seven**: the entry list is checked against the implementation's exported bodies in BOTH directions by the build and also derives the linker's export set, so the declaration is a THREE-copy contract; and the bridge does not call the ABI at all, it calls a mojom method that a renderer dispatch table maps to the export, so two more hops sit between them. **AND TWO SUBPROBLEMS UNDERNEATH WERE UNBUILT**, which the scope had silently asserted were done — there was no receiver for a partial delivery anywhere, so the second chunk would have died at a landed abort. **THE LANE'S REFUSAL WAS THE DELIVERABLE**, and it is the outcome to want: a diff that cannot be coherent within its permitted scope is not landed narrowly and is not escalated as a risk — it is decomposed, and the ordered subproblems are named. The tell for whoever writes the brief: you named the two ENDS of a seam and no hop in between, which means you drew the path from what the components are FOR rather than from what they CALL. **AND THE OTHER WAY A SCOPE LIST GOES SHORT IS THE OPPOSITE FAILURE, WHICH LOOKS LIKE DILIGENCE: TRACING THE ROUTE ONE HOP AT A TIME.** The tell above catches a path drawn from what components are FOR. This one is drawn from what they CALL — correctly, by reading, at every step — and it is still short, because **a hop-by-hop trace terminates at the first file that HAS what you need and never asks whether THAT file's inputs are born there either.** Each reading is honest, each is verifiable, and each produces a scope list that grows the moment somebody reads one hop further. Measured across three rounds on ONE subproblem, every round a refusal with evidence: the answer went from two directories, to two plus a struct plus five call sites, to that plus three more file-pairs — the third only because a lane traced the ALGORITHM'S OWN INPUTS instead of the next call. **THE CURE IS TO ENUMERATE WHAT THE ALGORITHM READS, FROM ITS OWN PROSE, AND ASK OF EACH VALUE WHERE IT IS BORN** — that list is finite and the standard states it, so it terminates in ONE pass where a call trace terminates at an arbitrary depth. And the corroboration is usually already in the tree: the file holding the last hop had **predicted its own arrival in writing**, saying the field was not carried because "each arrives with its first consumer" — so the design knew where the boundary was and only a reader tracing inputs would meet the sentence. The tell that you are about to do this: your scope list grew last time you read further, and you have not asked what the algorithm's INPUTS are. **AND WHEN A LANE HANDS BACK ITS ORDERED SUBPROBLEMS, A DEPENDENCY ORDER IS NOT A LANDING ORDER — AND THE LIST OUTRANKS THE PROSE UNDER IT.** A decomposition is written deepest-first, because that is how you discover it: the thing you cannot build without is named first. A LANDING order is the reverse of that whenever the deep members have no consumer yet, and §A-FIELD-A-CONSUMER-DEFAULTS already says why building one is building nothing — a write with no reader is untested code wearing a completed subproblem. **THE FAILURE IS NOT THAT THE LANE FORGOT TO SAY SO. IT IS THAT A NUMBERED LIST IS AN INSTRUCTION AND A SENTENCE BENEATH IT IS NOT.** Measured, and the coordinator was me: a lane returned `(a)..(d)` with the words *nothing before (d) has a reader* written directly under it, I read that report, agreed with it in writing, and dispatched (a) — because `(a)` is where a list starts. The lane came back having established that the consumer I claimed was waiting does not exist, that four candidate readers all take a different type, and that my suggested fallback was forbidden by a documented decision one file over which exists to prevent a leak my route would have re-introduced. Every one of those was findable before the dispatch. **SO A DECOMPOSITION STATES ITS LANDING ORDER IN THE NUMBERING ITSELF, or it is a trap for the next reader — who is usually the person who asked for it and is skimming.** Write the member that HAS a consumer as (1) even when it depends on the others, and say which of them must land WITH it; a set that cannot land except together is ONE landing and is numbered as one, never as four. And the receiver's cheap check, which costs one question and would have saved this: **for each member, name the CALL that will consume it and confirm that call exists today.** Where the answer is none, that member is not first however deep it is. **AND WHERE THE SHAPE HAS BEEN DELETED BEFORE, THE TREE SAYS SO AND THE SCARS ARE THE FIRST THING TO GREP** — here two of them, an entry removed by name and a wire ordinal recorded as retired and NOT REUSED, each naming the reason a second door drifts. **AND THE PARAGRAPH YOU ARE READING FAILED TO PREVENT ITS OWN REPETITION, WHICH IS THE PART WORTH KEEPING.** The incident above is recorded with its subject (a chunk-delivery ABI entry), its wrong number (three) and its right one (SEVEN). Months of readers later, the same coordinator scoped the same entry — a chunk-delivery ABI entry — at **FOUR** files, and the lane traced it to seven again: header, implementation, `build.mjs`'s entry list AND its exported-functions set, the renderer document's binding table, the mojom method and ordinal, the bridge, the chokepoint. **THE RULE WAS PRESENT, SPECIFIC, AND ABOUT EXACTLY THIS TASK, AND IT DID NOT FIRE.** The reason is the form: an incident is written in the past tense with a protagonist, so it reads as HISTORY — evidence for a principle already absorbed — rather than as a CHECK owed on the task in hand. A reader scanning for principles finds the principle and stops; the one paragraph in the file that names their exact artifact never surfaces, because they were not searching for an artifact. **SO THE HABIT IS TO GREP THIS FILE FOR THE SUBJECT BEFORE SCOPING A DIFF, NEVER FOR THE PRINCIPLE** — `ABI`, `gitlink`, `realm`, `census`, whatever the diff is actually about — because the incident that names your artifact is worth more than the rule that generalises it, and it is the one you will not think to look for. The tell is that you are writing a scope list at all: a scope list is a claim about a call path, and this file has probably already been wrong about that path in your exact area. **AND THE CHEAPEST WAY TO GET THAT LIST WRONG IS NOT TO REASON ABOUT THE PATH AT ALL — IT IS TO RUN THE RIGHT SEARCH AND COPY OUT A TRUNCATED ANSWER.** Every other rule here about a bad scope assumes the author DERIVED it — from what components are FOR, from a recollection, from a summary. This one is derived from a command run correctly, minutes ago, against the real tree: the search was right, the pattern was right, and a DISPLAY LIMIT cut it. **THE RESULT IS A FLOOR AND IT READS AS A TOTAL**, which is §A-COUNT-OVER-SOURCE-TEXT's defect arriving through how much of the answer you LOOKED AT rather than through how you spelled the question — and it is worse in one way, because the spelling problem at least leaves you suspicious of your regex while this one leaves you certain, having just watched the tool answer. **AND THE RELAY THAT CARRIES IT IS TRUTHFUL, WHICH IS WHY NOBODY CATCHES IT**: "I ran this myself just now and got hits in A, B, C, D, E" is an accurate account of what the author saw, and what they saw was capped. Measured, by me, in a brief: a five-file scope went out for a Fetch subproblem, cut at twelve matched LINES rather than twelve files, and the file it omitted was the one holding the residual the whole task was about — the lane found it by re-deriving, which is the only reason it cost a paragraph instead of a diff. **THE TELL WAS PRINTED IN MY OWN OUTPUT AND I DROPPED IT IN TRANSCRIPTION.** The tool said, in as many words, that it was showing a limited result; the brief did not. That is the general shape and it outlives this tool: **an instrument that truncates SAYS SO, and a relay is where that sentence goes missing**, because a list is copied and a caveat beside it is not. So carry the limit with the list or do not carry the list — and when a search feeds a SCOPE rather than an example, ask it for the COUNT of files instead of the rows, since a number cannot be silently cut and a listing can. The tell that you owe this: you are about to paste search output into a brief, and you did not look at whether there was more of it.
- **A PRIVATE INDEX IS BANNED, AND THE PROCEDURE THAT USED ONE DELETED THE REPOSITORY.** Work happens in the ONE checkout. **WHAT IS BANNED IS LANDING THROUGH A SECOND INDEX, NOT HOLDING A SECOND CHECKOUT — AND THIS SENTENCE SAID `WORKTREES ARE BANNED` FOR LONG ENOUGH TO CONTRADICT THE FREEZE PROCEDURE §A-GATE-RUN-FROM-THE-WORKING-TREE PRESCRIBES IN THIS SAME FILE.** The incident below is entirely about a WRITE PATH — `hash-object`/`update-index`/`commit-tree` against an index at a `$$`-named path — and the absolute it was written as reaches a READ-ONLY snapshot that shares none of that machinery. That over-claim is §AN-OVER-CLAIM-IS-REFUTABLE performed on this file's own reader: one counterexample sits ten bullets down, wearing the words `so a gate runs from a FROZEN SNAPSHOT`, so a reader who greps the ban finds the file disobeying it and is invited to discard the part that matters — never land through an index git did not put there. Measured: an agent freezing a snapshot read both sentences, silently arbitrated the contradiction, and picked its own third answer; it happened to pick the better one, and nothing about the file made that outcome likely. **SO THE LINE IS DRAWN BY WHAT THE SECOND TREE MAY DO, NEVER BY WHAT IT IS CALLED:** a checkout that is only ever READ — built, scanned, measured — is the prescribed instrument and needs a name; a checkout anything STAGES OR COMMITS FROM is the trapdoor, whatever it is spelled as. And prefer `git clone --shared` over `git worktree add` for the read-only one, because the preference falls out of that same test rather than out of taste: a worktree SHARES the parent's `.git`, so its index, its refs and its `worktrees/` metadata are reachable from inside the thing you froze to be unreachable, and a clone's are not. The frozen-worktree-plus-`hash-object`/`update-index`/`commit-tree` landing this file used to prescribe is gone, because it had a trapdoor nobody could see: `GIT_INDEX_FILE` named with `$$` is a DIFFERENT PATH IN EVERY SHELL, and each tool call is a separate shell — so staging wrote one index and `git write-tree` read another, ABSENT one. **`git write-tree` on an absent index does not fail; it returns the EMPTY TREE `4b825dc6`**, and `commit-tree` commits it. That is exactly what happened: one commit deleted EVERY FILE IN THE REPOSITORY on BOTH refs, and it was reported as landed and verified. Use ordinary `git add <explicit paths>` and `git commit` — never `git add -A` or `git add .`, which sweep whatever else is in a shared tree — and check `git status` before committing so another agent's work in progress is not swept into your message.
- **AND IT IS NOT WHERE WORK IS READ EITHER, BECAUSE NOBODY CHECKS IT OUT — SO IT IS ALWAYS STALE AND `git diff HEAD` IS SILENT ABOUT IT.** The rule above closes the write; the read is the half that keeps landing. Every lane lands through a private index and nothing advances the shared HEAD, so that HEAD sits dozens of commits behind `origin/main` and every file under it is a version that was true once. The tell is MECHANICAL and it is the reverse of the usual one: `git status` and `git diff HEAD` are QUIET or report a file as merely "modified", while `git diff origin/main` is loud — because the tree is BEHIND, not ahead, and a diff against the wrong base cannot tell those apart. Three measured this session: an agent's `git ls-files` called two committed files untracked and it nearly reported a lane's landed commit as one that does not compile; a lane's working copy of `test_forced.c` was stale by two other lanes' commits, so `git diff origin/main` showed **−48 lines** and staging it would have reverted both under a message about ECMAScript §12 — and that lane wrote down that `git diff HEAD` would have shown nothing wrong at all; and a stop-hook reported "uncommitted changes" every turn for files whose blobs were byte-identical to landed commits. **DIFF AGAINST `origin/main`, NEVER AGAINST `HEAD` — UNLESS THE CHECKOUT IS AHEAD, WHICH IS THE CASE THIS RULE ORIGINALLY GOT BACKWARDS.** The reason for the rule is a checkout BEHIND the remote: there `git diff HEAD` is quiet or reports a file as merely modified while `git diff origin/main` is loud, so staging what `HEAD` shows silently reverts other lanes' landed commits. The symmetric case exists and inverts it. When a peer has COMMITTED LOCALLY and not yet pushed, the checkout is AHEAD, and `git diff origin/main` then contains that peer's whole commit — so a lane obeying this rule literally would sweep it into its own hunks, which is the same defect this rule exists to prevent, arriving through the rule. Measured, and caught by the lane before it staged: `origin/main` moved twice during one turn, a peer's 55-file commit was local-only when the lane started, and diffing against the remote would have taken all of it. **SO THE FIRST COMMAND IS NOT A DIFF, IT IS A RELATIONSHIP**: `git merge-base --is-ancestor origin/main HEAD` and `git rev-list --left-right --count origin/main...HEAD` say which of the two you are in, and BEHIND and AHEAD take opposite bases. Never assume the direction from the fact that a peer is active — both directions have been measured in this repository within one hour, and before hashing any shared-tree file confirm `git diff origin/main -- <file>` contains ONLY your hunks. Before believing a shared-tree file at all — a grep, a line number, a "this function does not exist" — READ IT AT `origin/main` (`git show origin/main:<path>`), because a claim about the tree derived from a stale checkout is a claim about a program no revision contains. And a file that differs from `origin/main` is not therefore unlanded: check whether its blob equals some landed commit's (`git rev-list origin/main -- <path>` and compare `git hash-object`), because stale-behind and work-ahead look identical in `git status` and only one of them is yours to save.
- **VERIFY A PUSH BY CONTENT, NEVER BY POINTER.** `git ls-remote` confirms a SHA, and a SHA says nothing whatever about a tree — the empty-tree commit above PASSED an `ls-remote` check and was reported as proof. After pushing, read the tree: `git ls-tree -r <sha> --name-only | wc -l` against the count you expect, and confirm the blobs you changed are actually present at that sha. The same rule holds before committing: a `git diff-index --cached` run in one shell says nothing about what a `write-tree` in the next shell will see.
- **Do subproblems IN ORDER** — never jump to a problem before its subproblems. If a shortcut was taken, remove that code.
- **Aggressively delete non-perfect code, riskiest first** — that shows you care how the system works; dead-code removal takes no risk and shows nothing. Delete anything a browser engineer or solver engineer would not fully endorse.
- **A LEGACY FALLBACK IS A `DFAIL`, NOT A REPORT.** Logic not designed for suspend/resume that still runs is an unbuilt capability, and the file already has the mechanism for that: it CRASHES at the site, naming what to build. Do not invent a register, a hit counter, or a harness line that lets the suite stay green while a non-suspending path keeps running — that is choosing a green result over the forcing function, and it is the same risk-avoidance as keeping the legacy body. The conversion sequence ends in the DFAIL: widen the selector, build what the failures name, DELETE the body, and the C entry becomes `DFAIL("… reached its C entry with an ITERABLE source — route that call site")`. If several directories go red at once because the fallback is still reached, that is the correct state and the work queue, not a reason to build a softer mechanism. A site that is a genuinely DIFFERENT algorithm (Array.from over an array-like: length+indices, no @@iterator) is not a fallback at all and must not be tracked as debt — mislabelling it manufactures an obligation to delete something that should stay.
- **A REJECTED OR INTERRUPTED TOOL CALL MAY HAVE ALREADY RUN — verify the tree, never assume.** A compound command (`git checkout … && gcc … && …`) can execute its first stage before the rejection lands. Twice this has destroyed committed-quality work whose loss was then REPORTED AS "the revert didn't happen" — a false claim about the state of the repo, which is worse than the lost work. After any rejection, interruption, or surprising result: `git status` / `git diff` / `grep` for the symbol you believe you changed, and state what the tree ACTUALLY contains. The same rule covers every "same place / already fixed / not a regression" claim — CHECK THE DIFF, never a snapshot of the current file, which shows where a line IS and proves nothing about where it WAS.
- **A PROCESS PATTERN MATCHES THE SHELL THAT NAMES IT, SO `pkill -f`/`pgrep -f` OVER A COMMAND STRING IS SELF-REFERENTIAL — ADDRESS A PROCESS BY PID.** The argument is the pattern, so the pattern is IN the argument list of the process doing the matching, and of every wrapper between it and the kernel. It has bitten in both directions in one session and the two failures look nothing alike, which is why one rule has to cover both: a `pkill -f` took out ITS OWN compound command mid-sequence, loudly, leaving the tree in a state the caller then had to reconstruct under §A-REJECTED-OR-INTERRUPTED-TOOL-CALL; and an `until ! pgrep -f <cmd>` wait-loop matched its own shell and therefore NEVER FIRED, silently, so a job that had finished long before read as still running and the work waiting on it simply did not happen. The silent direction is the dangerous one, because a monitor that never fires is indistinguishable from a job that never ends, and the mistake is invisible in the output of the very command that embodies it. **THE DISCRIMINATOR IS TO LIST RATHER THAN TO MATCH** — `ps -eo pid,args | grep <pattern> | grep -v grep`, read the rows, and act on the PIDs you actually meant; a count from `pgrep -c` answers a question about a pattern and not about a job. This is the same defect as the loaded-machine one, moved into the tool: an artifact of HOW you asked, reported as a fact about WHAT you asked.
- **AN EXIT STATUS BELONGS TO THE PROCESS THAT PRODUCED IT, AND BACKGROUNDING INSERTS A PROCESS — SO A COMPLETION NOTICE FOR A BACKGROUNDED JOB IS A STATEMENT ABOUT THE WRAPPER.** The rule above is about ADDRESSING the wrong process; this is about BELIEVING one, and it is the same family as verifying a push by pointer: a proxy answered, the answer was well-formed, and it was about something else. A shell that backgrounds a job and prints its PID exits the instant it has done so, successfully, whatever the job goes on to do — so the harness reports the job "completed, exit code 0" while the job is still compiling, or has already exited 1. Measured: a build was launched that way, the wrapper's 0 was reported as the build's, and the build had in fact exited 1 on a missing toolchain seconds earlier — two processes, two statuses, and the one that got reported was the one that could not fail. **THE 0 IS THE DANGEROUS DIRECTION BECAUSE IT READS AS SUCCESS AND IS ALWAYS AVAILABLE**, and it does not stop at a wrong belief: acting on it started a SECOND concurrent build, which is precisely the state §Testing's one-builder rule exists to prevent, so a misread status manufactured the condition that rule is about. It also produces the emptiest possible artifact — a "successful" build that compiled nothing — and §MEASURE-WHAT-THE-SHIPPED-PATH-WRITES already says an absent result and a zero result are different facts that must never be averaged. **THE DISCRIMINATOR IS TO WAIT ON THE PID AND THEN READ THE JOB'S OWN ARTIFACT** — `while kill -0 <pid>; do …; done`, then the log it wrote and the file it was supposed to produce — never the status of the thing that launched it. A job's exit code is worth having; it is just never the one the launcher hands you. **AND A PIPELINE HANDS YOU ITS LAST STAGE'S STATUS, WHICH IS THE SAME SUBSTITUTION WITH NO BACKGROUNDING IN SIGHT.** `ps … | grep <pattern> | head` succeeds because `head` succeeded, whatever `grep` found — so a `|| echo "NONE RUNNING"` written after it is UNREACHABLE, and the branch that exists to report an ABSENCE is the one branch that can never run. The absence then reads as whatever the last line happened to be, which is the §MEASURE-WHAT-THE-SHIPPED-PATH-WRITES pairing again: an absent value and a cleared value are different facts, and this construction averages them silently. Measured, in the check that was supposed to confirm no second builder was running. The cure is not a longer pipeline: put the DISCRIMINATING command last, or read `PIPESTATUS`, or drop the pipe and test the captured output — and never write a fallback whose condition a downstream process owns. **AND THE PRESCRIBED CURE CAN ITSELF BE DELIVERED THROUGH A PROXY, WHICH PRODUCES THE MOST CREDIBLE WRONG ANSWER THIS DEFECT HAS.** The rule above ends in a remedy — wait on the PID, then read the job's own artifact — and that remedy is a LOOP, so the reflex is to run it in the background too. The instant you do, the thing that exits immediately is the launcher, and its 0 is reported under the WAITER'S name. A bare job's wrapper at least reports under the job's name, and a reader who distrusts it goes and looks; a notice bearing the name of the process whose ONLY PURPOSE is to answer “has it finished” is an attestation, and nobody re-checks an attestation. So the cure is not a construct, it is a habit: after any completion notice, ASK THE KERNEL ABOUT THE PID (`kill -0 <pid>`) and look for the job's own stage processes before believing it. Measured, minutes after the parent rule was being re-read: a build was launched, a `while kill -0 8861` waiter was started in the background to watch it, the harness announced the WAITER complete with exit 0, and `kill -0 8861` answered ALIVE while `emcc` was visibly compiling. **THE TELL IS ARITHMETIC AND IT IS FREE: A WAITER WRITTEN TO POLL FOR MINUTES THAT “COMPLETES” IN SECONDS HAS NOT WAITED FOR ANYTHING.** Compare the notice's elapsed time against the shortest run the job could possibly have, and where they disagree the notice is about a different process. The general form outlives the shell: **a verification delivered through the same mechanism it was written to distrust verifies that mechanism and not the subject** — so a check's transport is part of the check, and asking who the answer is ABOUT is owed even when, especially when, the answer arrives wearing the checker's name.
- **PUSH BACK — sycophancy is a design failure, exactly like a defensive fallback.** Agreeing because something was asserted (by me, you, or the existing code) rather than because it is SPEC-TRUE is the same evil as swallowing a crash. When a claim conflicts with the spec (ECMAScript/WHATWG) or the real code, say so and prove it (`JSON.parse(location.hash)` throws `SyntaxError` in V8 — the honest answer, never a fabricated reason it doesn't). Deferring to authority over spec is a bug. Treat positive feedback as always-to-be-worked-on. Approximations are worthless. Avoid the word "genuinely" — it is a cop-out.
- **Own the push, AND PUSH TO BOTH REFS — a push to `main` alone is REVERSED by the next merge of the session branch.** Commit + push VERIFIED work yourself, to `main` AND to the session's designated branch (`claude/…`), every time. They are ONE line of history, not a trunk and a fork: the session branch is where the work is reviewed and merged FROM, so a `main` that is ahead of it is a `main` whose extra commits the next merge silently undoes. This is not hypothetical and it is not small — the session branch sat 280 commits behind `main` with ZERO commits of its own, so every landing of an entire session existed only on the ref nobody merges. It was a strict ancestor, which is the ONLY reason it cost a fast-forward instead of a reconstruction. **VERIFY BOTH REFS AFTER PUSHING** (`git log --oneline -1 origin/main` and the same for the session branch); a push that reports success for one ref proves nothing about the other, and the two-ref state is exactly what nobody thinks to look at. Before starting subagents, SYNC FIRST and confirm both refs agree — several agents committing onto a ref that is already behind multiplies the divergence by the number of agents. Engine = a clean quickjs-ng-delta fork (submodule `engine/qjs`, branch `apiclient-v2`, synced to upstream `quickjs-ng/quickjs`); the host/scheduler lives OUTSIDE the submodule (`engine/host/main.c`) to keep the fork a minimal delta. Build: `node engine/build.mjs`.

# Offensive programming — the mechanism is `engine/host/check.h` (mirrored on the JS side by `extension/check.js`)
Always safe to ship DCHECKS! If crashing thats a blocking issue to support properly. Pre-existing flaws must be fixed when order is right.
- **Every error/crash/abort in a DEV build is CORRECT, never softened.** It is exactly one of: (1) an UNEXPECTED STATE — an invariant the design must guarantee, eliminated at the ROOT so it cannot arise; or (2) a FEATURE THAT SHOULD EXIST but does not yet — BUILT at the root as a proper system. NEVER caught / swallowed / logged-and-continued / clamped / `?:`-past, never wrapped in try/catch for "browser-faithfulness". A crash is the system doing its job (forcing the root fix). A page's own uncaught throw aborting boot is INTENTIONAL — the engine has not yet faithfully implemented that capability; build it at the root. In release we cannot fix or add features, so the same error is ALSO correct (it fails because the capability isn't supportable outside dev); the release exemption never licenses a dev-mode fallback. **BUT THE ARM BENEATH A `DFAIL` IS A SHIPPED CODE PATH THAT NO GATE HERE CAN EXERCISE, AND TWO COMPONENTS' RELEASE ARMS OF ONE ALGORITHM CAN COMPOSE INTO A STATE NEITHER OF THEM CONTEMPLATED.** The sentence above settles whether the release arm is ALLOWED; it says nothing about whether the release arms of one algorithm AGREE, and that is a different question with no instrument behind it. The dev build never reaches them — the `DFAIL` fires first, which is the whole point — so every gate in this tree is structurally blind to the composition, and the arms are written by two authors each reasoning correctly about their own half. Each one alone is a DEFINED wrong answer, which §Fetch-§5.2's residual rightly prefers to an undefined one; TOGETHER they are a state the algorithm has no step for. **AND THE FAILURE LANDS SOMEWHERE ELSE ENTIRELY, WHICH IS THE PART THAT COSTS THE MOST.** Measured across §8.4 Dynamic markup insertion: one file's release arm took its spec's own "return document", leaving a Document with **no open stream**; the sibling file's release arm then ran its own step 10 into that Document, reached the parser's chunk entry outside the stage that accepts one, and died at an **always-fatal allocation `CHECK`** — so a production crash reported MEMORY as its cause for a defect that is a missing stream. That is §A-QUESTION-SOME-ENTRIES-ASK's "an UNRELATED SUBSYSTEM failing on input it should never have been shown", arriving through the release exemption instead of through a dispatch, and a plausible diagnosis is worse than silence. **THE RULE IS THAT A RELEASE ARM IS PART OF THE ALGORITHM AND IS DESIGNED WITH IT, NEVER CHOSEN LOCALLY**: when you write one, name the other components that take a release arm of the SAME algorithm and say what state the pair leaves behind — and where the pair is incoherent, the downstream half GUARDS ITS OWN PRECONDITION rather than trusting a sibling's arm. That guard is not a fallback by §C-stack's test: delete the thing it selects against and the question still has to be asked, because it is the algorithm's own precondition. The tell is greppable and cheap — **a `DFAIL` whose release arm RETURNS SUCCESSFULLY rather than throwing**, since a throw ends the algorithm and a quiet return hands the next component a state it will never test for.
- **`DCHECK(cond,msg)` / `DFAIL(msg)`** = DEV-ONLY should-never-happen (design invariant, not-yet-built capability, COW-host gap). Aborts loud at the origin emitting `@WHY` with cond+file:line; compiled out in release (`-DAPICLIENT_DEV=0`) — condition MUST be side-effect-free, never recoverable control flow. Asserts "the engine's OWN logic is correct."
- **`CHECK(cond,msg)` / `CHECK_FAIL(msg)`** = ALWAYS fatal (dev AND release, emits `@E`) for a universal invariant that must hold in production: OOM/allocation (a dropped flow corrupts the frontier), a physical RAM/disk floor, a security/authorization boundary, data integrity. Asserts "we must not PROCEED even in production." When unsure, it is a DCHECK. **BUT A DIFF THAT MAKES A DCHECKED POINTER LOAD-BEARING IN RELEASE HAS SILENTLY PROMOTED THAT DCHECK, AND FAILING TO FOLLOW IT TRADES A DEV ABORT FOR A RELEASE SEGFAULT.** The choice above is made once, against the invariant as it stands; this is the obligation a LATER diff creates, and it is invisible because the assert reads unchanged while the code beneath it does not. The trigger is mechanical: a DCHECK guards a pointer, the pointer is dereferenced only on dev-only paths, and your diff adds the FIRST release-mode dereference of it. The check is then compiled out in exactly the build where the dereference now happens — the guard and the thing it guards have been separated by the edit rather than by anybody's decision. Measured: a rung write added at a delivery seam became the first release dereference of a flow pointer a `DCHECK(f != NULL)` had been covering, and was upgraded to a `CHECK` in the same diff. So when you add a dereference, ask which build the guard above it survives in — and if the answer changed because of your line, the promotion is part of your line.
- **Assert EVERY invariant EAGERLY at its origin; too FEW asserts is the failure.** A bad state caught late is a bug you built on; caught where born it is a fix. Never a `?:`/`if(bad)return`/clamp/default past a broken invariant; never a try/catch that swallows one. Migrate every raw `abort()`/hand-rolled `@E`/`@WHY`/`assert` to the right macro. The goal is ZERO `@WHY`, reached by fixing the root — never by muting the check. DCHECKs are always safe to ship (no production effect). NOT a `@WHY` (do not assert these): a forced-exec flow THROWING on opaque/attacker input, and an unsolved `@S` sink (a parked search) — those are the exploration surface, not broken invariants. Learnings from a non-best design are worthless — known-incomplete logic is still a should-never-happen DCHECK. **AND AN ASSERT WHOSE OWN MESSAGE CONCEDES THE CASE BENEATH IT IS SOUND IS GUARDING AN EXPECTATION, NOT AN ERROR — THAT WORD IS THE CHEAPEST TELL IN THIS FILE.** A `DCHECK` asserts this codebase's own logic is correct, so a message admitting that the state it fires on is legitimate has refuted itself in the sentence a reader is standing in. Measured, and the crash said it outright: an operand reaching a fork seam without a spellable identity aborted under text reading "with no key its completions are re-forked at every ask rather than replayed, **which is sound** and is not what a machine declaring a fork expects". Six sites in the same component treated that NULL as a designed state — one returning early by name, one minting the sibling under a named mechanism, two spelling it into their own messages — and the decisive one was a SECOND SEAM over the identical primitive that ran the same three calls with no assert at all. **TWO SEAMS OVER ONE PRIMITIVE, ONE ASSERTING AND ONE NOT, IS A DISAGREEMENT, AND THE SILENT ONE IS USUALLY RIGHT** — it has been exercised on the wider population without firing, which is evidence, while the guard is one author's expectation about a narrower path. That is the consumer-outranks-the-declaration rule with both sites being callers. So when an assert fires: read its MESSAGE for a concession before reading the code for a cause. "which is sound", "this is correct but", "harmless yet", "not what X expects" — each says the author knew the state was legitimate and asserted anyway, and each turns a hunt for a bug into a two-line deletion. AND THE DELETION IS NOT THE WHOLE DIFF: a guard that stood for a while has usually been CITED, so grep for the argument it retires. Here a neighbouring comment justified its own early return by "the walk above already DCHECKed its key", which the deletion made false in the same instant — the retired-argument rule arriving from inside the same file. **AND THE DISCRIMINATOR THAT DECIDES THIS IS WHOSE BYTES STATE THE VALUE, WHICH IS A QUESTION ABOUT EVERY ZONE AND NOT ONLY THE ENGINE'S.** A `DCHECK` asserts "this codebase's OWN logic is correct", so it may only ever stand on a value THIS CODEBASE COMPUTED; a value a stranger's server stated is not an invariant that can be violated, it is INPUT, and the answer to a malformed one is a REFUSAL yielding the record's declared absence — never an assert, and never a default that turns the absence into a plausible datum. The two failure directions are not symmetric and both are live: asserting on foreign bytes hands a remote party an abort switch for the TRUSTED zone, while defaulting past them is §A-FIELD-A-CONSUMER-DEFAULTS. Measured, in the trusted zone rather than the engine: a target's own error reply — a `google.rpc.Status` this project never produced — reached a store record whose shape check is a `DCHECK`, so `{"metadata":{"service":7}}` from any third party fired OUR assertion mechanism and aborted the zone that holds the network policy; alongside it, a `(constructor)` where a proto type belongs made the parsed `type` the `Object` CONSTRUCTOR, which `structuredClone` refuses, so ONE probe answer poisoned the store and stopped every popup open for that profile. Neither is a field-name contract, so the record-field gate is structurally blind to both — its own banner says a zero over a boundary with no producer in the corpus is "silent about that boundary, not clean about it". So: at every boundary, ask who authored the bytes; refuse what a stranger stated, assert only what you computed, and where an assert guards a value YOU wrote, assert its TYPE and not its content — a path this codebase composes is legitimately empty, and demanding non-empty re-introduces the remote abort switch one field over. **AND IN THE SOLVER HALF THE FOREIGN BYTES ARE THE SUBJECT MATTER, SO THE BOUNDARY THAT WOULD REMIND YOU IS NOT THERE.** Everything above asks who authored the bytes at a place where the question is obvious — a reply arrives over a network, a record is parsed, and the seam announces itself. The @S components have no such seam: their input is THE ANALYSED PAGE'S OWN SINK OUTPUT, which is exactly the thing a forcing solver exists to be handed, and which is attacker- or page-controlled BY CONSTRUCTION. An assert there is a page-held abort switch on the whole engine, and it is easier to write than the network one precisely because nothing in the file looks like a boundary — the bytes arrive as an ordinary `const char *` from a component one directory over. **AND THE INPUTS THAT REACH IT ARE NOT EXOTIC, WHICH IS WHAT MAKES IT A PRODUCT DEFECT RATHER THAN A CURIOSITY.** Measured on a locator-placement refusal, three shapes an ordinary bundle emits every day — a duplicate attribute, a doctype in body position, an unterminated tag — each reaching the abort. A page does not have to be hostile to hold that switch; it only has to be sloppy, and real sink output is sloppy. So in the @S half the rule from §WHOSE-BYTES-STATE-THE-VALUE is read one step harder: the derivation may assert what IT computed — its own tables, its own enum kinds, its own invariants — and it may never assert anything about what the parse FOUND, however malformed, because §Solver-half already fixes the answer for that case and it is not a crash. **An unsolved sink is a PARKED SEARCH, which §Offensive-programming names by hand as explicitly NOT a `@WHY`** — so "I could not place the locator" and "no escape leaves this state" are first-class RESULTS the caller records, and a component that aborts instead has converted the exploration surface into a broken invariant. The tell is greppable and takes one question: **read what the asserted value was derived FROM, and if the chain reaches the document's own bytes, the assert is wrong however sound its reasoning.** **AND THE ASSERT THAT CANNOT FIRE AT ALL IS WORSE THAN EITHER, BECAUSE IT REPORTS AS A PASSING CHECK AND IS NOT A CHECK.** Everything above is about an assert standing on the WRONG VALUE; this is one whose two sides cannot disagree, so it holds under every state of the program including the one it was written to forbid. It is not a weak guard — a weak guard fires sometimes. This one is a NON-check wearing the syntax of a check, which is the §A-FIELD-A-CONSUMER-DEFAULTS defect performed on the verification layer: a plausible pass is indistinguishable from a real one, and the more prominent the assert the more thoroughly it certifies what it never examined. **THE WAY IT COMES ABOUT IS PLACEMENT, WHICH IS ALSO THE TELL**: an assert written where its subject is NOT YET IN SCOPE cannot name that subject, so the author reaches for the literal they just copied from it — and a literal compared with the constant it was copied from is a comparison of a thing with itself. Measured: two guards of a hand-copied forward alias read `ALIAS == 68` and `ALIAS == 64` under messages saying the alias must not drift from its parent, and each sat one line ABOVE the parent's own definition, which is the only reason the literal form was written; they passed for every renumbering of the parent, which is the single drift their own messages name. So an assert NAMES BOTH OPERANDS, and where one is not in scope the assert MOVES to where it is rather than being weakened to fit — and when you meet one, ask what value of the program would make it fail, because if you cannot construct one the assert is the finding. **AND AN INVARIANT OVER A GATED OPERATION CENSUSES THE ASK, NEVER THE OUTCOME — OTHERWISE IT RE-IMPLEMENTS THE GATE'S OWN REFUSALS AS FAILURES.** This is the shape that makes an `owed ⇒ done` assert either impossible or trivial, and the difference is one choice of recording point. Where the work passes through a gate that legitimately declines — an exposure test, a security-context test, a permission — a census of what LANDED cannot tell a component that never asked from one the gate correctly refused, so the assert fires on every correct refusal and is abandoned as unbuildable. Record instead at the CALL, before the gate, and the invariant becomes independent of every arm the gate has: the question is no longer *did this end up present* but *did anyone ask for it*, which is a fact about this codebase's own components and therefore the only kind an assert may stand on. **THE GATE'S CONDITIONS THEN STAY IN EXACTLY ONE PLACE**, which is what stops the assert becoming a second spelling of them that can drift. Measured: an `owed ⇒ present` census over Web IDL §3.8 would have fired on every Window-only interface in a worker realm and every secure-context interface in an insecure one — both correct — and moving the recording point one call earlier reduced the unasked count to zero in both realm kinds while leaving a real historical defect firing. **THE TELL THAT YOU ARE ABOUT TO GET THIS WRONG: your census reads a RESULT — a property that exists, a file that appeared, a row that landed — and the operation that produces it has any arm that may decline for a good reason.** The general form is one question asked before choosing the recording point: *what does this instrument do when the system correctly does nothing?* **AND WHERE THE RECORDING POINT CANNOT MOVE, THE ZERO IS TWO STATES AND THE REPAIR IS A SECOND FIELD RATHER THAN A BETTER PLACE — MOVING IT WOULD CHANGE WHAT THE NUMBER MEANS AND LEAVE THE OLD MEANING UNREAD.** The rule above is about an ASSERT and prescribes moving the recording point ahead of the gate, which is right because an assert's subject is the ASK. A METRIC's subject is usually the guarded population itself, and then the same move is not a relocation but a substitution: the quantity you get afterwards answers a different question, and the one the field was built for has no reader at all. So an observation site sitting inside a guard leaves its metric's `0` meaning two things at once — *the site was reached and read zero* and *the site was never reached* — which is the ABSENCE-and-ZERO-read-alike defect arriving in the instrument rather than in the subject, and it is invisible because a fraction with no denominator renders identically in both. **THE MOVE CAN ALSO SIMPLY FAIL, WHICH IS WHAT MAKES THIS A RULE AND NOT A PREFERENCE**: where the guard is one of several regimes the component runs under, hoisting the counter resolves one reading and leaves the others exactly as merged as they were. Measured at TWO independent sites in one engine, each reasoned out by its own author without reference to the other and each reaching the same answer: a walk counter left counting WALKS PERFORMED rather than hoisted past its gate, because hoisting would have given the number a different meaning and left the old one unread; and a replay fraction whose observation site is the single line that consumes a recorded arm, guarded on the flow carrying a payload, so a search never offered an arm and a search refused at its first arm are byte-identical — and those two take opposite work, one being a question about whether a recorded path existed and the other about what stands in front of its first branch. **THE REPAIR BOTH SITES CHOSE IS TO PUBLISH THE DISCRIMINATOR BESIDE THE NUMBER**, never to relocate the number: a REACHED bit set at the first observation, or the REGIME stated by whatever emits the pair, after which each reading is nameable and the field keeps the meaning it was built for. **THE TELL IS THAT YOUR METRIC IS A FRACTION WHOSE NUMERATOR IS PUBLISHED AND WHOSE DENOMINATOR IS NOT**, and the cheap question is the one this file already asks of every count: what does this read when the instrument was never asked at all? If the answer is the same digit it reads when it was asked and answered nothing, the second field is part of the first one's diff. **AND THE SECOND HALF OF THAT INCIDENT IS THE SAME DEFECT IN PROSE, WHICH IS WHY THE TWO BELONG IN ONE RULE: A COMMENT BINDS TO ITS SUBJECT BY POSITION AND BY NOTHING ELSE.** §AN-INDEX-NAMES-A-THING-ONLY-WHILE-THE-SET-IS-FIXED and §THE-FILTER-ITSELF-HAS-AN-ORDINAL are about a NUMBER that names a member by rank; this is the same binding with no number in sight, and it is the commonest ordinal in any codebase. N notes stacked above M declarations bind by ADJACENCY, so writing them in the opposite order to the things they describe silently re-points every one — no compiler sees it, no grep finds it, and each note reads as authoritative about the declaration it now sits over. Measured in the same file as the vacuous guard, and it cost a reading: two notes stacked above two defines in reverse, so a note explaining why one kind is DISTINCT from another sat above the alias that IS that other kind, and read as a claim that the shared number was a collision. The repair is the general one — put each note UNDER the single thing it describes, so the binding is one-to-one and an insertion cannot re-point it — and the tell is a run of consecutive declarations with a block of prose above them: ask which note belongs to which, and if you have to count, so does everyone else. **AND A CRASH IN THE `default:` ARM OF A SWITCH OVER A VALUE THIS CODEBASE COMPUTED IS A GUARD, NOT AN UNBUILT CAPABILITY — SO A COUNT OF CRASH SITES SUMS TWO POPULATIONS THAT TAKE OPPOSITE WORK.** §Offensive-programming gives a `DFAIL` two meanings, an unexpected state and a feature that should exist, and the second is what makes a crash a work queue. A `default:` over a closed enum is neither: it is the first meaning discharged correctly, the arm is UNREACHABLE by construction, and the right action is to LEAVE IT ALONE. Both spellings are the same macro, the same file, the same grep — so any tally of “how many crashes does this component owe” is the three-states-behind-one-answer shape, and it errs in the direction that manufactures obligations. **AND THE CHECK THIS FILE ALREADY PRESCRIBES PASSES ON IT, WHICH IS THE WHOLE REASON IT NEEDS ITS OWN SENTENCE.** §A-BRIEF-WRITTEN-OUT-OF-A-CRASH-MESSAGE says to grep the coordinate at `origin/main` before it enters a brief, and that rule is aimed at a claim that has gone STALE. Here the coordinate is perfectly current: four crashes, four exact line numbers, all present at the revision you are about to dispatch against. Grepping confirms the crash EXISTS and is silent about whether it names WORK. Measured, one command from a dispatched brief: four `DFAIL`s reading “§4.10.5.1's convert a string to a number was asked of a state that defines none” sat in `default:` arms of switches over an input-state enum, under messages ending “`input_number_applies` is that list and every caller has it in hand” — a well-built file, read as four gaps. **THE DISCRIMINATOR IS ONE QUESTION AND IT IS FREE: WHO ENUMERATES THE OPERAND.** A value THIS codebase enumerates (an internal enum, a class id, a tag) makes the arm a guard; a value the SPEC enumerates (a state the algorithm defines, a type from a fetched document, a destination) makes it a gap, because the standard can name a member the engine has not built. And the message states which, if you read it rather than counting it: a guard's text names its own unreachability proof — the predicate, the list, the caller that holds it — while a gap's text names what to build. That is the mirror of the concession tell above: there, an author admitted the state was legitimate and asserted anyway; here, an author proved the state impossible and a READER counted the proof as work. So before a crash becomes a lane, READ THE SWITCH IT BELONGS TO, not just the line it sits on.
- **AN ASSERT THAT NAMES A REMEDY BUT NOT A SITE IS A CRASH NOBODY CAN ACT ON, AND A SHARED HELPER IS WHERE THE SITE GOES MISSING.** A DCHECK stamps the file and line it is WRITTEN at, so an invariant checked INSIDE a helper reports the helper for every caller — one line for every transition in a file, one line for every read in a tree — and the message's remedy ("route this read", "route this call") then names an action with no object. **THE REASON THIS SURVIVES IS THAT THE CRASH READS AS COMPLETE**: it states a correct spec fact and a correct instruction, and only when someone tries to obey it do they discover there is nowhere to apply it. That is the stale-`DFAIL` failure mode with the coordinates missing instead of wrong, and it is worse in one way — a wrong file sends you to the wrong place, where you at least find out; no file sends you to read hundreds of call sites, which nobody does, so the crash is rediscovered instead of fixed. It has been rediscovered four times here, at two scales: a coercion and a property read each reachable from a thousand-odd C callers, and, one level down in the same session, two transition helpers whose six and three callers shared one reported line. Every cure was identical and every discovery was independent. **SO THE SITE TRAVELS WITH THE OPERATION** — a `__FILE__`/`__LINE__` pair captured at the CALLER and threaded to the check, never derived at the helper, and never captured at an intermediate that would name one forwarding function for the whole tree. **AND AN INVARIANT OVER A TRANSITION IS A MACRO EXPANDED AT EACH SITE, NEVER A FUNCTION CALLED FROM THEM**; a wrapper introduced to "share the check" re-creates the defect it was reaching for. Internal callers pass a NAMED sentinel rather than a null, so a caller that was never converted cannot masquerade as one that has nothing to say. The tell is mechanical and worth asking before writing any should-never-happen: **count the call sites that can reach this abort — if that number is larger than you would read by hand, the ADDRESS is part of the assert, not decoration on it.**
- **Confident change = small components × two-sided asserts** — ONE system, not two. An isolation-testable component is a narrow interface you exercise with ONE fixture AND whose invariants crash loud when violated. Without the asserts it silently corrupts; without the boundary the asserts have no contract. Split every over-large file until each piece is one assertable contract; give each component its own DCHECKs. Metric: can you exercise this with one fixture AND does a violated assumption crash at its origin?
- **A FIELD A CONSUMER DEFAULTS IS A FIELD NOBODY WILL NOTICE IS NEVER WRITTEN, and the default is not the symptom — it is the concealment.** `x || 0`, `x || []`, `x || {}`, `x ?? d`, `x?.y`, a `catch {}` around a read, an `if (kind === N)` on a value the producer never returns: each turns "the producer does not produce this" into a plausible datum, and a plausible datum is indistinguishable from a measurement. SEVEN instances surfaced in ONE session, every one a live product defect and not one of them visible as a crash. `qjs_result` had NO CALLER and `result || {}` made that `{success:true}` with zero endpoints — a false clean bill from a run that learned nothing. `bridge.js` branched on `st === 1` for NEED_FETCH while `qjs_step` returns only DONE(0)/YIELD(2), so the whole `qjs_pending → safeFetch → qjs_provide` reply path was UNREACHABLE in the shipped extension and every flow a page's `fetch()` parked stayed parked forever — §Attacker-sources calls learning from replies "the POINT", and it had never once happened. The popup read `cspBlocked` where `solve.c` emits `cspBlocks`, so EVERY finding badged HIGH and a breakout real Chrome's CSP kills was reported as a clean XSS. `canVerify` required a `shape` field carrying a `{hash}` vocabulary the engine has never spoken, so live verify rendered for ZERO findings ever emitted. `resolverErrors || []` dropped every engine-recorded page error (`pageErrors`). `_orphans`/`_work`/`_parked || 0` were three names nothing emits, so the interleave diagnostic read zero forever. `@RESUMED` had a reader and no writer, so the number written to prove the cold tier read its residue back was `0` for every session there has ever been. **THE TELL IS GREPPABLE AND THE RULE IS MECHANICAL: a name that is READ somewhere and WRITTEN nowhere is a broken contract, and a default is what stops it being a crash.** So a consumer NEVER defaults a producer's field — it DCHECKs the field's presence and shape (`extension/check.js` mirrors `check.h` for the JS side), and a value the producer can legitimately omit is a POSITIVE statement the consumer reads as one (absence of `cspBlocks` IS `policy_allows` returning true), never a hole a `||` fills. When you add a reader, grep for its writer; when you rename a field, the old name must not survive on either side. **AND THE TWO HALVES CAN CONCEAL EACH OTHER, WHICH MAKES REPAIRING EITHER ONE ALONE A REGRESSION RATHER THAN A PARTIAL FIX.** The rule above treats read-with-no-writer and write-with-no-reader as two defects; they can be ONE, and the pair is invisible in a way neither half is. Measured: a lookup composed its key in a shape the only mint never produced, so the arm behind it could not run — and that arm's body wrote a field whose name occurs EXACTLY ONCE in the whole tree, at that write, with no reader anywhere and a record constructor that REJECTS it by name. Each half made the other unfalsifiable: the field was never read because the writer never fired, and the writer never firing went unnoticed because nothing downstream missed the field. **Repairing the key alone would have begun writing a constructor-forbidden name onto live records that the persistence layer carries into IndexedDB** — a regression delivered as a fix, and one that outlives the session that shipped it. So a dead path raises a SECOND question before it is revived: what would it have done had it run? A path nobody has executed is not working code, it is untested code, and reviving it is a behavior change owed the same scrutiny as writing it. The tell is cheap — grep every name the dead arm WRITES, not only the one that made it dead. **AND THE SHARPEST FORM OF THIS IS A CONTRACT THAT NAMES A HAZARD AND OFFERS NO EXIT FROM IT, WHICH DOES NOT WARN CALLERS AWAY — IT GUARANTEES THEM INTO IT.** Every rule above is about documentation that is WRONG or MISSING; this is documentation that is exactly RIGHT and still causes the defect, which is why no reviewer catches it: the comment reads as diligence. An accessor that tells its callers "a caller that wants a DISTANCE TRAVELLED must not take this number — it is the denominator, fixed from the instant the blob is built" and then exposes no way to take the other number has not protected anything. The one thing a caller can do is the forbidden thing, so the forbidden thing is what every caller does, for the life of the API, while the contract sits above it saying otherwise. Measured: a census row was fed the chain LENGTH for its entire existence under a header claiming it was the position, and the two files disagreed with the correct one being the accessor nobody could obey. **THAT IS `comments are not valid for follow ups` ARRIVING THROUGH A COMMENT THAT IS TRUE** — a warning a caller cannot act on is an UNBUILT CAPABILITY wearing prose, §Offensive-programming's category (2), so it crashes or it gets built and it is never merely written down. The tell is greppable and takes one line: **a comment of the form "callers must not use X for Y" beside an interface that offers only X.** Ask, at every such sentence, what the obedient caller is supposed to CALL — and if the answer is nothing, the sentence is the bug report and the missing entry is the fix. And make it a separate ENTRY rather than another out-parameter on the same one, because two answers of different KIND taken from one call are free to be read into a variable named for the other, which is the same defect one argument over; separate entries make that mistake a different CALL instead. **AND THE FORM THAT IS IMMUNE TO REVIEW IS A FILE THAT STATES THE RULE CORRECTLY AND THEN VIOLATES IT IN THE SAME PARAGRAPH.** The sentences above are about a contract with no exit; this is a contract with a correct ARGUMENT and a wrong APPLICATION sitting together, and it survives longest, because the site reads as ALREADY AUDITED. A reader arriving at a paragraph that names the hazard, explains why it matters and cites the rule does not then check whether the code beneath it obeys — the reasoning IS the evidence of diligence, so scrutiny stops exactly where it was needed. Measured: a fitness ladder's own banner condemned the previous design in the words "a candidate 800 gates into its replay and one that has not yet reached its own SOURCE READ both stand at exactly 0", and then introduced a ladder whose bottom rung is observed AT the source read, of which that same sentence is true. §@S(i)'s requirement — every rung has an observation site strictly BEFORE the thing it is a distance to — was quoted accurately four lines above a rung that does not meet it, and the lane that found it said why nobody had: the file reads as though the question was already settled. **THE TELL IS COUNTER-INTUITIVE AND IS THE POINT — THE PROSE IS UNUSUALLY GOOD.** A paragraph that argues its case at length is one whose author was thinking hard, which is exactly the condition under which a rule gets stated in the abstract and applied to the wrong operand; so the reflex to trust a well-reasoned banner is the reflex to check it. Read the rule and the code as TWO claims and test the second against the first yourself. A file may not be its own auditor, and the better it argues the less it is one.
- **A PREDICATE THAT ANSWERS TWO QUESTIONS IS DECIDED BY THE STRICTER ONE, AND THE COST LANDS SILENTLY ON THE OTHER.** Two facts about one thing get ONE bit because at the site that introduced it they agreed; later a case arrives where they DIFFER, the bit keeps whatever the stricter question needs, and the looser question is refused with nothing anywhere to say it was ever asked. This is the defaulted-field defect with no field in it: the refusal is a real answer to a real question, so it reads as correctness rather than as loss, and no name is missing for a grep to find. **THE TELL IS A REFUSAL WHOSE STATED REASON IS ABOUT A DIFFERENT QUESTION THAN THE CALLER'S** — an exclusion justified by "X states its own extent, so a member it lacks is not unknown" standing at a site asking "may the walk descend through X and NAME what is inside it", which are not the same question and do not have the same answer. So when a predicate refuses, read its reason against the CALLER's question, and where they are two questions, split them into two predicates over the ONE bit: the bit is a FACT and each predicate is a QUESTION asked of it, which is also what stops the split becoming two bits that can disagree. Measured: a server's injected state is records inside LISTS, and one membership bit meant every record inside a list was unreachable and answered `undefined` — while the bit that would have named it was already set on it.
- **AND THE SAME DEFECT ARRIVES THROUGH A CONSTANT RATHER THAN A BIT: A DEFAULT THAT IS GENUINELY ARBITRARY AT ITS SITE IS NOT ARBITRARY IN EFFECT WHEN THE THING IT INDEXES IS NUMBERED BY SOMEBODY ELSE.** The rule above is one bit answering two questions; this is one CONSTANT selecting into two namespaces, and it is harder to see because the site is HONEST — it says the choice is arbitrary, and within its own frame it is: nothing was observed, so something had to be picked, and picking is stated rather than hidden. What the site cannot see is that the ARM NUMBERS mean different things depending on who spelled the predicate.
